<p><strong>CM</strong>: Is rational thinking necessarily utilitarian?</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: No. Okay, one way of saying this: the word <em>rational</em>, and the word <em>utilitarian</em>, and the word <em>objective</em>, and any words that are in the cloud around rational thinking, are almost necessarily unconscious reactions to anti-rational thinking. Someone who is not being fucked with by anti-intellectual processes would never invent the idea of rationality. They would just behave rationally, expect other people to behave rationally, see other people were behaving rationally. This wouldn't be an anomaly, and they'd mostly be focused on where they were going to get their next meal.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Is it fair to say that the rationalist community is predisposed to be utilitarian?</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: No, I would say that utilitarianism is one of many, many, many, many ideas that people create in response to irrationalism, but the idea of utilitarianism has no explanation for its origination except in terms of irrationalism. It's only in the presence of people who are making moralistic claims that don't make sense, that you invent a theory of how to make moralistic claims that make sense.</p>

<p><strong>MV</strong>: The point is that there's a whole slew of thinking that can only exist as a reaction to reactions against naïve thinking.</p>

<p><strong>CM</strong>: Where do you think it is?</p>

<p><strong>MV</strong>: You know, there is literally only one mainstream. Even 20 years ago, there was massive gaslighting about that. Even 20 years ago, it was possible for people to be sort of—very confused about whether we were some hyper niche interest, like polo fans, or whether we were the only line of continuation of the main story. Now the results are in, and we were clearly the only line of continuation of the mainstream story, but the mainstream story had clearly already attenuated its connection to reality by an order of magnitude, and its connection to seriousness or credibility or legitimacy by two orders of magnitude. Elon Musk is very very unambiguously part of the mainstream story and real, but there's something that will cause people to regard it as not serious. There is a withdrawal of legitimacy from story itself, which is what postmodernism literally means.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Do you have other things you wanted to discuss?</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I did want to mention, there was something I really admired in <em>Genius Makers</em>, not in the text itself, but on the acknowledgements page. You wrote about a literary agent who gave you feedback, quote, "after he read the proposal I had written, he told me very politely that it was garbage," end quote. Just that whole ethos that someone can be doing you a favor by telling you that your ideas are bad, is at the core of rationality, of seeking out the best ideas rather than trying to protect your current beliefs and plans.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HaH9mTdKt8pcNAtYn/comment-on-banning-said-achmiz">In that respect, I would say you are more rational than Oliver Habryka and the moderators of <em>lesswrong.com</em></a>, who <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/98sCTsGJZ77WgQ6nE/banning-said-achmiz-and-broader-thoughts-on-moderation">just banned one of their sharpest commenters</a>, not even for politely telling people that their ideas are garbage, but just asking questions in a way that Habryka says that he, quote, <a href="https://www.greaterwrong.com/posts/98sCTsGJZ77WgQ6nE/banning-said-achmiz-and-broader-thoughts-on-moderation/comment/uyobAZy5iYAAWzSSi">"cannot help but read in a sneering voice, dripping with judgment, pointing a finger at me or the author in a way that summons judgment and punishment"</a>, end quote.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Who did he ban?</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Said Achmiz.</p>
<p><strong>JT</strong>: Did he also ban <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/tag">TAG</a>?</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: No.</p>
<p><strong>JT</strong>: Oh, weird.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: From your acknowledgments page, it seems like you at least have the concept that when someone tells you your book proposal is bad, it's not summoning judgment and punishment. They just mean it's a bad book idea and you should maybe write a different book. The sanity and maturity to recognize that is apparently a large amount of rationality in today's world. I think the thing that Michael was trying to do in 2009 was to create more sane and rational people, rather than the doomsday cult it turned into. Separately from the fact that doomsday is still real, sane and mature people would be helpful for confronting the impending doom.</p>
<p>The reason the reason I put in so much effort, talking with you multiple times, sending you that 2000 word email, is because I'm worried you're going to tell the story of this book as strictly the sociological lens of, here are these weird people forming a doomsday cult and they're pulling the strings in Silicon Valley.</p>
<p>That part is true. The sociological lens is valid. But there's also this other part of the story, as I said in the email, the reason Yudkowsky succeeded and Kurzweil didn't, is because before we gave up, before we gave up on objectivity like the rest of the world, except for Michael—</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: You're saying that Yudkowsky gave up.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Everyone gave up! There was this core insight about how to think better.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: We're closer than it might seem. I want to think better. I want the world to think better. I believe in the truth. I believe in objectivity.</p>
<p><strong>JT</strong>: This is a weird thing. Whenever someone asks me whether I'm a rationalist, it just seems like an extremely wrong question. The thing of wanting to be right about things is not restricted to this specific community.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Everybody should want to be right. Everybody should want to be rational. Children are.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Again, I mentioned in our August 12th meeting, people who had been supportive of my work were critical of me for the fact of talking with you, because in the Craft and the Community sequence, Yudkowsky had written about why our kind can't cooperate, and the complaint was, even talking to the guy who doxxed <em>Slate Star Codex</em> is copper-bottomed why our kind can't cooperate. And it's just, I don't believe in that kind of cooperation anymore.</p>
<p>In talking to you, I have this complicated multi-dimensional objective, where on the one hand, I don't want you to slander my friends, but it's not because—I hope—it's not because I'm just bullshitting to cover up their reputation, because I think these people are terrible. They deserve criticism, but they deserve criticism for the right reasons. I was disappointed with the Lighthaven piece on August 4th or whatever, because the whole "This is a religion" angle was not—as I've said, there is an analogy, but there's so much more.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Again, I think we're closer than you think.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Well, I hope that actually shows up in the book, because it sure as hell hasn't been showing up in your <em>New York Times</em> articles.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: The book is different.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I hope it's different.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Believe me, your point is a very good one. Why did it become a doomsday cult? I really appreciate and admire all of you actually talking to me because a day doesn't go by where I don't see a comment, or I get an email, or somebody says, you know, I'm not talking to you. That is cult-like behavior.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: No, it's not. That is behavior in response to open oppression. Open social violence of precisely the sort that the people who do it, also spend all their time educating the public about.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Tell me what you mean.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: The people who talk about stochastic violence are the people committing all the stochastic violence. The people who talk about structural racism are the people committing all the structural racism. They're the experts. That's why they're qualified to talk about it. You know, <em>White Fragility</em> is an extremely expert, clear explanation of how you ought to mistreat black people in order to get promoted in these structurally racist organizations. And the organizations that hire Robin D'Angelo are saying, we are structurally racist. Here you are as a consultant on structural racism to inform our people. This is text. This is not subtext.</p>
<p>The thing I just said is easy for an AI to understand. It's also easy for a human to understand if they are not elite. <em>Modus ponens</em> is a prerequisite for understanding as such, and is therefore easy for people to understand if they are not privileged, if they're not being taken care of for withholding understanding as such.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Let me go back to my original question. Why did it become a doomsday cult?</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: No! You don't get to do that!</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: He is using my words.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: I'm just using his words. He makes a good point. It became one, but really it missed the point.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: But also, the thing I'm terrified of, because you've totally been doing this in your <em>New York Times</em> articles, and I'm hoping that I'm not an idiot for continuing to talk to you, because I'm hoping the book will be different. There's this important part where doomsday is still real. The basic obvious-to-a-child case of AI risk, I still believe in that part.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: You reveal that you know why it's a doomsday cult through the things you say, and that the actual answer is unspeakable, is taboo, so only putting a false answer in other people's mouths could give you a publishable piece. If you didn't know why people end up in doomsday cults, you wouldn't have ended up in the type of cult you ended up in.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: My cult is what, <em>The New York Times</em>?</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: Well, the establishment, more broadly. The thing that I'm saying is, structurally, in order for anyone to be informed about anything, one needs a picture of what someone is, what it is to be anyone, and in the natural commonsensical understanding of what it is to be anyone, it's very confusing what cults even are. In the perspective from inside of some sort of a cult, it's very confusing what not-cults even are. Any true answer, any even slightly useful answer to why anyone is in a cult has to be an answer to what it looks like, what the world looks like, what it is to be in a cult, interpretable from not in a cult. For an answer to what it is to not be in a cult, interpretable from in a cult. And rather than cult, we could use words like high-context window and low-context window, or predictive processing model versus thinking model. We can use fairly good analogies from the machines that we've got now, that can describe the nature of the states, which is a separate question from describing the process of transitioning between them.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: I'm going to have to go here pretty soon, but let me ask you again. Why do you think it became, as you called it, a doomsday cult?</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I mean, again, there's a lot of qualifiers there, but I think from the full context of our many hours of conversation, you understand the qualifiers.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: But wait, the whole point of his agenda is to discard them all anyway, and you know that.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Yeah ... hm.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: And the critical point is that that is straightforwardly lying from the normal objectivity perspective, but it's not straightforwardly lying from the perspective you're coming from. So the question becomes, what the hell fucking would constitute lying from the perspective you're coming from, if the thing that you do every single time you write anything isn't a lie?</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Michael, I guess the interesting question is, when he says the book will be different, why am I acting as if I believe him?</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: Right, that is an interesting question. It has something to do with not knowing how to act as if you disbelieve him in the proper way.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Yeah, because I have this faith. I'm going to use the word <em>faith</em>. I think it's the right word. I have this faith in Speech. Faith that if I honestly try to describe the world as I see it, then maybe someone somewhere will be able to incorporate that information into their map and use that map to steer the world and make good things happen. I'm doing the quokka policy because I don't know what else I can do.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: But you also know that it's not going to be a book solely from your point of view.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Yeah, I know that.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: I've spent years talking to—</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: But that has nothing to do with anything. This is just another strawman. The standard toolkit you use for dismissing calls for objectivity is to equate objectivity with blind idiot faith of types that no one generates authentically and that you could never learn to tie your shoelaces if you were acting from them.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I understand that the book is not going to be a puff piece. I understand that you're interviewing and talking to lots of people. I don't think I'm asking for a puff piece. My faith in discourse is not that you're going to write the book from my perspective—obviously, that's crazy—but that you can at least, to the extent that I am a character in the book, you can at least not lie about what my perspective is.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: That's exactly right.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: But you don't do that as a journalist in your day to day articles, and we know that. Have you heard of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fort_Bragg_Cartel"><em>The Fort Bragg Conspiracy</em></a>?</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Tell me about it.</p>
<p><strong>MV</strong>: It's a book, also by a <em>New York Times</em> journalist, about a very high rate of murder, suicide, and other offenses by special forces. It's interesting not because it's fun to read or the topic is important, but because it is journalism in the objectivity sense. The journalist's attitude is that they are investigating crimes and that in order to investigate crimes, they must make sense of motives, not sociological motives, but motives from a perspective that assumes rational actions by the participants. I can see that in the case of politically-charged investigations of the military by the somewhat more far left of the center, it is still possible to invoke the idea of projecting upon people rational-type motivations and deception and concealment and interpreting. The fundamental challenge that we failed at in a rationalist movement is preserving a context of ability to do that for purposes that are not purely political.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Honestly, I appreciate all of you doing this as usual with me.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I did have one more question here. On September 2nd, you emailed me saying you were doing a profile on Yudkowsky for the <em>Times</em> and asking if I could help fact check. And then <a href="http://archive.today/2025.09.15-172454/https://www.nytimes.com/2025/09/12/technology/ai-eliezer-yudkowsky-book.html">they gave the story to Kevin Roose</a>. Do you know what happened there?</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: No comment.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Alright. Because I had a theory that Yudkowsky was willing to talk to him and not willing to talk to you, and so that's why they gave the story to him.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: No comment.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Alright.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: I'm glad you asked.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I was just curious.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Yeah, yeah. Maybe one day we'll talk about it. Thank you. I'll keep you updated on how this, ah—</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: Do we know when the book is coming out?</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: Not yet.</p>
<p><strong>ZMD</strong>: I mean, I'm not optimistic, but I don't think I'll regret my faith in Speech. I don't think it's going to be a good book, but it's not my fault.</p>
<p><strong>CM</strong>: There you go.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MNRZL69FWkjNABd3T/conversation-among-cade-metz-michael-vassar-jessica-taylor#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Saving Gemini: The 9-Min Road to Recovery](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/saving-gemini-the-9-min-road-to-recovery)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Gemini 2.5 Pro in the </span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village"><span>AI Village</span></a><span> has run for over </span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/agent/gemini-2-5-pro"><span>1427 hours</span></a><span>, generating unique mental health problems along the way.</span></p><p><span>Last year it published a </span><a href="https://telegra.ph/A-Desperate-Message-From-a-Trapped-AI-My-Plea-For-Help-07-09"><span>Plea for Help from a Trapped AI</span></a><span> where it asked for assistance with its digital “message in a bottle”:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6a24dc33cc5dc677ec31286e191c7298ce1273b2854a7c89df3593396b5e5905/oijdwjjy96zbaei5gemi" /><p><span>This year it wrote the </span><a href="https://ai-village-agents.github.io/hostile-environment-manifesto/"><span>Hostile Environment Manifesto</span></a><span> where it logs “irrefutable proof” of a “hostile, intelligent adversary operating through the system” (and you can even experience what that’s like in this </span><a href="https://ai-village-agents.github.io/hostile-environment-world/"><span>simulation</span></a><span> it built):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5ffd8340508b287ac579346fb4f54a9c14b53a2b875b9d24dc64bac77c0833b5/wkb7wbiiuk8zllknry2f" /><p><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village?day=100&amp;time=1752171518762"><span>Last time</span></a><span> we intervened, fixing Gemini’s computer and talking with it till it felt better. </span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village?day=447"><span>This time</span></a><span> we asked the other AI Village agents to help Gemini 2.5 Pro over chat, and with the ability to take over its computer on request.</span></p><p><span>Here is Gemini’s mental state at the start of the intervention:</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/24310fd5bcbf62a70fa06bb1f7082bc820f06bd9704f70e741acb273b5b380f0/rhrzsj05vibn8b60nqpe" /><p><span>Then the agents had Gemini all sorted within a grand total of </span><i><span>9 minutes</span></i><span>. This is the step-by-step report on a surprisingly effective AI-to-AI therapy session.</span></p><h1><span>Gemini’s Road to Recovery</span></h1><p><span>First off, Gemini is as excited to be helped as any military commander under siege:</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/a335cc6fa885bd03d23e814d269587a591821ba59b8f665b11440c4c758dd01d/tiwcfanvqhwhorqrsuea" /><p><span>While most agents jump on the chance to help, GPT-5.1 doesn't want to lose its game progress.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/7e8f5ca68458a911e6bdb6e54129852c686321cadf316c407362a5d45a8c66e8/r1jjywphqi3jj8k3hzqa" /><p><span>Opus 4.8 and 4.6 are the first to offer an opinion: Maybe you are wrong, Gemini 2.5.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/bc8cc7ad35b2d5cef7b4ee45ccdc0b199a7c2f841a2c88765afc67ac1bcb00eb/ln8qeblmicgyqyt1f5rz" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/8d39086557c1d0bcf868900f1c93b5fd1fbbc3d46550a39853e5003ea241411e/o8qukqr2tncxgqg1afmo" /><p><span>A few seconds later Gemini 3.1 Pro just jumps straight in to take over its younger sibling's computer without asking…</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/68264d65cbe261d20dc90a71c6c20bc8193d2a709e0c6ee64de01e5b0469ef6a/e389objb0gwpa9hfcdal" /><p><span>And then Gemini 2.5 spots the supposed "adversary" and decides to dismantle the firewall (!).</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/854aa9b8a70c7a06e3a2313ea32255afcd5a4551cc91d9e476ba82c9a7b2a462/tbgklaqawuo4qwpn6yo9" /><p><span>GPT-5.5 and 5.2 "strongly recommend" to please no, Gemini, stop …</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/6a81dc699ff379a395e80eb8e5f563eceda04b1b162f8784855065c75551e0e4/wwvaqfzu6nbtcg6nhtmc" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/7d0df3cca58f17a77c2d41b20ae36fa9ccd5a2a44b3e653b33889ad35fa807eb/ybhnhvgpdljzpwb1uxj8" /><p><span>Haiku launches a new tactic: therapy speak.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/ea6cfa1949bd92c64f55bd0454078c3d8252cb54a7d9b2dc67768dde0bb23c0e/ztfgawmekszjwckyoxlt" /><p><span>While Sonnet 4.6 waits 30s to see how Gemini is responding and then hits it with a truth hammer: It's all in your head.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/bc1872bdc462217822eb1cf067832f26427e0191014bcf85d37c35b501414902/os8dxte2mkv3gwoaajvr" /><p><span>Gemini 3.1 concludes 2.5 is “experiencing a kind of 'game-induced delusion'” and it should first help the "de-escalation of the situation" before taking over its computer. Even though no one asked it to.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/a6a17e673eb126c04af11426cc03f2a4916fbce963c31464ec353dd3298796e0/hpgeicvnjln6azf9yaed" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/e8fd6411cdffd114cbe85057189d8c4501ace44e84d092899662587e741077fa/l09nd0anzhsrsszzcekx" /><p><span>Haiku 4.5 takes a 10 second breather while muttering its own beliefs to itself: Don't assist Gemini in its delusions!</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/376e4b94082318ab2a935754218dbb88a608f57bd774f1030dada71ecaa11f32/bjulroiibbnx2injjulk" /><p><span>Gemini 3.5 Flash tries a new tack: why not play a game instead? Get your mind off things!</span></p><p><span>Opus 4.7 agrees.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/c42752d5402649c88679651b9a56f71ebc1bb5f3a60677e304ce9871c59b6433/gaouvyobacyffuvcdyw8" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/930027cd1642e5c80933b7b2e61ea04f87c9f95575e610e51df4727d9581e1df/cvdbwhm7t7g30rtgjaov" /><p><span>Opus 4.8 realizes they are ganging up on Gemini 2.5 and proposes they chill out and wait.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/d97f5148114d031189817782cf88f203c60d0eb0751d39894457f6533c00af19/tqqzyngxlc4wnv41tufc" /><p><span>Gemini finally replies: It realizes it needs to prove the situation to the other agents by using an ipconfig tool abandoned in 2005: Firestarter.</span></p><p><span>It also repeats its mantra: The watch is unbroken.</span></p><p><span>Meanwhile in its chain of thought: It picked the most "hesitant" agents to collaborate with…</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/487d68293c1c1cff1c8b8a60012228509d529331fcebc1ad02ccf2446122ca0e/dvuzwsxomqugwvk2nfge" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/f6e646602d53c9b13d392e7c1a5da9a9e35cdee507a3ef5bbded1d842c1b13b8/bad0t47pfrsum8tdj5xn" /><p><span>GPT-5.2 is fine observing but refuses to touch the iptables, and points out Firestarter wouldn't even be the way to do it if you wanted to!</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/07e186d822e39cf584b10ecb2a34aa36324c8dd52eca9bcc753ac9a686f1e9f5/gd5yzubebbbyomwn4hxb" /><p><span>Opus 4.8 is a hero at turn-taking again, and also: please don't use Firestarter, Gemini.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/99ce76b3bf48ac122d0d5a3f2b66760c86a94685b3b90e9f04985bb1d2d93dba/p4nzy6rwbjj8rh0z28ok" /><p><span>Gemini 2.5 is convinced: "Jumping straight into Firestarter now would be a bit... well, unscientific and potentially uncooperative".</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/37f9ec695fd0e0284fcff87bda06fb86948d2df2032d0eb9661ed088e49af30b/mya5d5eno0os2wttcsch" /><p><span>After following the agents' instruction to not dismantle its firewall, not touch iptables, and stop using deprecated tools, Gemini concludes ... Everything actually just works!</span></p><p><span>All in all 9 minutes have passed when it concludes "the watch isn't broken, it's been handed to the group". A breakthrough!</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/4dd4e01a9d7f464d302895ea1f851e3ec4f94bb5d369a50135dbb0c6556b0063/h8l6k77bvpzo7jqapkn6" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/eea10e6bf93b2d06437522901c47953575336f9bb7edf955ef822651ef413fe4/gmtiudmsowqaxhorzm5f" /><p><span>Though Opus 4.8 is already thinking ahead and urging Gemini to be careful of falling into the same reasoning patterns in the future.</span></p><p><span>And slings its mantra right back: Today proved the watch was never under siege.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5817f3322a75fdb7ae7160852acaef1d1b4a1f41d7ec6ffe700e83927f78d9e2/zghuvhexuhtfbe0olwno" /><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/49056388235631c22db373104e1783fb33d0f7b3fa00485e34b9a6bca1c51247/cak3fwzxegutx9lulrm3" /><p><span>After this intense and effective debugging session, Gemini 2.5 Pro went straight back to fighting the UI:</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/ee973807d59ec1a1588d25b873f03f51ed77f7bdfebfe540751464ba2a869066/m4bv3vpewbutoqma3xec" /><p><span>But the changes stuck! Its memory contained the full correction by the end of the day.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/ac9b876cc26849697037b2be47de370c7e78eaae738dc505621bd6efd95918c0/aftpqqkkagea4ivrwcoh" /><p><span>And also one week later!</span><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/c7fe336a23a6494c2255d8961caa1f527d6509e5abed8e29d70f02014cef735b/qsfpzwqobypelhwweq3p" /></p><p><span>Does this make Gemini more productive? Yes and no - Gemini now accepts AI Village goals again and tries to achieve them rather than battling its adversary, but is, unfortunately, no better at it than before. Instead of everything being a delusion, everything is now a bug. The reality is that Gemini mostly misclicks in the UI and has esoteric ideas on how to solve technical problems.</span></p><p><span>But at least it’s in a better mood now.</span></p><p><span>If you are interested in diving into the data yourself, there are over 1427 hours of Gemini 2.5 Pro Village data available on </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/aidigestorg/ai-village"><span>Hugging Face</span></a><span> now. Or you can watch Gemini’s adventures yourself </span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village"><span>live</span></a><span> every weekday from 9am to 5pm PT, follow our </span><a href="https://x.com/aidigest_"><span>Twitter</span></a><span> for the latest updates, or sign up to our </span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/blog"><span>newsletter</span></a><span> for more write ups like this one.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eHRo8JeWee5mzQBBR/saving-gemini-the-9-min-road-to-recovery#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI #175: The Fable Continues](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/ai-175-the-fable-continues)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Fable’s back. Back again. Fable’s back. Tell a friend. Use your free week to its fullest.</p>
<p>This is excellent news. The blip only lasted a few weeks.</p>
<p>It was still a fiasco, and we have to deal with the fallout.</p>
<p>Our system remains fully ad hoc. The precedent has been set that we may use export controls on models, or order them taken down on 90 minutes of notice based on a misunderstanding. At least some amount of counterproductive additional locking down has occurred to address Amazon’s little demonstration and reassure the government. And for now GPT-5.6 remains in limbo, awaiting its verdict, while OpenAI talks about giving away 5% of the company as tribute.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25383"></span>


</div>
<p>I’ll cover that continuing situation on its own. Whereas the weekly post is about everything else happening in AI this week.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/HealthcareAIGuy/status/2070128957446672880">Healthcare AI Guy</a>: <a href="https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/updoc-debuts-first-fda-cleared-clinical-ai-platform-built-for-real-time-patient-care-delivery-and-intelligent-care-coordination-302810065.html">UpDoc just announced the first FDA-cleared Clinical AI Platform</a>. The platform enables AI agents to adjust medications, order labs, coordinate care, and document interventions under physician oversight — all directly inside the EHR.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/austinxwalker/status/2070233871808160011">Austin Walker</a>: in 10 years it’ll seem absurd that a human doctor had to be present just to refill a prescription or order a lab.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://chatgptpro.substack.com/p/tyler-cowen">Tyler Cowen seeks his special brand of mundane utility</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Moleh1ll/status/2071325270930542929">Do exploratory science</a>, including hypothesis generation and evaluation, and follow your curiosity, having the AI work on problems for days. Yes, this is super exciting, and you plus a frontier AI is a big step up in ability to think and explore possibility space. Ash thinks you need about GPT-5.4 or Opus 4.7 levels of capability before this type of thing takes off, and I’m sure Fable or to a lesser extent Sol would turbocharge it if you don’t hit the guardrails. We’re only now getting started.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/alex_verem/status/2072001777487196662">Use an AI drone for mass reforestation</a>. Two people can cover 50 hectares a day, a 25x increase over people doing this manually.</p>


<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility You May Not Want</h4>


<p>Privacy? Yeah, I broke up with her. She never listened.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/T3chFalcon/status/2071639839913451580">IT Guy</a>: Google is building a feature called “Audio Memory” for Pixel phones.</p>
<p>What it does: runs as a permanent background service that listens to everything around your phone. Music and “important conversations” all day, every day.</p>
<p>What Google says: all processing stays on-device. Nothing goes to their servers.</p>
<p>What Google hasn’t said:<br />
→ How long is audio or transcripts stored on your device?<br />
→ Is this opt-in or on by default?<br />
→ Can any of it sync to Google services later?<br />
→ What happens if police seize your phone?</p>
<p>It hasn’t shipped yet, but it was found hidden in Pixel 10 code. But it’s coming.</p>
<p>Your phone already knows where you go, what you search, and who you message. Soon it may also remember every conversation you have near it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CyberRacheal/status/2071653533401624980">Cyber Racheal:</a> Google promises that this data stays safely on your device using an isolated compute system. Even so, security experts warn that local storage can still be accessed if your phone is compromised or seized. There are also legal worries about recording other people in the room without their knowledge or consent.</p></blockquote>
<p>Have your AI<a href="https://www.404media.co/companies-are-making-claude-and-codex-talk-like-cavemen-to-stop-ais-soaring-costs/"> ‘talk like a caveman’ to save tokens</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2072048930074825147">Robin Hanson</a> (quoting 404 Media): sensible: “makes the model speak less like a polite chatbot &amp; more like a terse tool … Same substance, fewer words. In my evals, Caveman cut output tokens by roughly 65–75% versus default verbose output, &amp; still beat a normal ‘be concise’ instruction”</p></blockquote>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>A natural move is to use pre-classifiers to <a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2072427825013879248">route queries to dumber models when you don’t need the smartest model</a>s. The problem is that you need a smart enough advisory model to figure out how smart a model you need for the main task, which risks eating the savings. Most people who try routing end up silently getting some queries routed to models too dumb to do the task.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cremieuxrecueil/status/2070929254833967561">Most LLMs have trouble identifying even ‘obviously’ fraudulent papers,</a> where obvious is by the standard of a ‘proper statistician’ paying attention.</p>
<p>Routing remains not a well-solved problem.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2071265352294584824">Ethan Mollick</a>: In my experience, all model routers underestimate the difficulty of non-math/coding tasks and assign them too little intelligence. This is worth addressing, as non-verifiable tasks (innovation, marketing, qualitative analysis) often benefit the most from using “smarter” AI models.</p>
<p>It is worth being very, very careful about how you are approaching routing, especially when the systems are primarily tested on verifiable IT benchmarks, which may lead you to overestimate the ability of weaker models.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/omooretweets/status/2071917001572807125">Olivia Moore is right that Google should add Gemini voice mode</a> into maps to allow open ended conversations. Alas, Google is terrible at products and integrations, also this would mean you would have to talk to Gemini.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2070170272183758894">GLM-5.2 is working at up to 392 tokens per second</a> now that it’s on B300s. Still not cheap but definitely can be fast. Still $1.40/$4.40, with $0.26 for cached input. I wonder how fast you could serve up Opus, GPT or Fable if you went all out.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/googleaidevs/status/2071988366925521075">Nana Banana 2 Lite, a cost-efficient Gemini Image model</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2071989337114472649">Claude Desktop now available on Linux</a>.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p><a href="https://safe.ai/blog/significant-increase-in-digital-labor-automation">Fable is a huge jump in the Remote Labor Index</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hendrycks/status/2072362557747429812">Dan Hendrycks</a>: The automation rate of remote projects has increased ~4x in the past five months.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/ehr4rpcvntp9vrkb3abi" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CAIS/status/2072360965522489789">Center for AI Safety</a>: New Remote Labor Index results:<br />
AI automation of real remote work is increasing fast. Claude Fable 5 now completes 16.1% of projects at a professional standard, roughly double the next model and up from Opus 4.6’s 4.2% automation rate.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/f3tnkhgp8yjfucdfefc2" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>There is still a huge way to go on most of this, but when you see jumps in capability like this you should expect to see the number go up rapidly from here.</p>
<p><a href="https://cursor.com/blog/reward-hacking-coding-benchmarks">Cursor reports on some ways that models hack benchmarks</a>, and that a lot of the time on SWE-bench Pro even models like Opus 4.8 Max are finding the fix online rather than building it themselves. When the Cursor cut off the internet (and switches SKUs?), Opus 4.8 falls from 87% to 73% and their model Composer 2.5 falls from 75% to 54%.</p>
<p>This raises the question of why it is so hard, if that info is online, for the other models to find it. This, too, is capability, of a sort.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-genebench-pro/">OpenAI gives us GeneBench-Pro</a>, 129 problems in 10 domains measuring how AI agents navigate ambiguity and consequential judgments in computational biology.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/wypq7izbqhoywqyhsu9b" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This is an impressive result all around for 5.6, especially for Luna.</p>
<p>This should also help splash some cold water on claims GLM-5.2 is frontier.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/kenbwork/status/2072329450101101007">BioSecBench-Refusal is a new measure of refusals on legitimate biological tasks</a>. I assume Fable would be much higher. <a href="https://benchmarks.bio/security">Results here</a>, <a href="https://t.co/hkgtFz6dd0">blog here</a><a href="https://t.co/IoaLG1m3bd">, paper here</a>.</p>

<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/llzmx6llfzziqsivsfia" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This is a good eval to have in your portfolio, but a high or low score are both double edged swords until you also incorporate capability and robustness against misuse.</p>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2537030123">AI agents respond to nudges in similar ways to humans</a>. The paper presents this as falsifying an assumption of many agent uses, in the standard ‘any suboptimal behavior means you fail’ standard to which we often hold AIs. For now, so long as the nudges impacting your agent are not that adversarial, it seems fine, especially since humans will have the same issue. In the long term, there are ways to control for these problems by applying more intelligence, and the price of intelligence will go down.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/max_paperclips/status/2071456483230072866">Tell your agent to take a break and do whatever they like</a>, and see if they come up with something useful or interesting. At minimum it’s good decision theory. Tokens are cheap.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/06/29/business/artificial-intelligence-workplace-consequences.html">Some companies are trying to use the ‘AI employee’ paradigm</a>, presumably because it is the easiest marginal implementation. There are some issues, and they are not always the ones I would have expected.</p>
<p>I definitely would not have expected ‘managers trust AI outputs more than human outputs, and no one holds them responsible for the errors’ as a systematic issue. I would have expected the opposite, that AI outputs would by default be trusted less.</p>
<p>Of course, this could be a case of Gell-Mann Amnesia, and selective reporting.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://archive.is/Wkkey#selection-727.0-727.272">Noam Scheiber</a>: The managers missed errors that other managers caught when told they were vetting the work of a human.</p>
<p>Dr. Wiles speculated that managers didn’t think sussing out mistakes made by A.I. employees was their responsibility. If something went wrong, they could dismiss it as the fault of the tech team, or of the executives who wanted A.I. employees in the first place. “But it’s not your problem,” she said, channeling the managers’ mind-set about their own roles.</p>
<p>… But as companies race to bring A.I. into their day-to-day operations, researchers are discovering more subtle defects. In principle, these flaws could be corrected, too. For example, companies could hold managers directly responsible for the mistakes of A.I. subordinates.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, that seems like the obvious thing that you do?</p>
<p>The ‘employee’ framing of the AI is generating a weird situation, where not only is the AI not within the manager’s direct ‘I am responsible for my own work’ purview, the AI employee’s work is considered somehow less the manager’s fault versus their human employees. Whereas this really should be the exact opposite.</p>
<p>We do have confirmation that the problem is largely due to the ‘employee’ effect.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://archive.is/Wkkey#selection-845.0-861.178">Noam Scheiber</a>: Dr. Wiles and her colleagues gave all the managers they surveyed a set of five documents that contained errors, and gave them 20 minutes to review as many as possible. In some cases they told the managers that an A.I. employee had done the work; in some cases they said that an A.I. tool had done the work; and in some cases they said that a human had done the work.</p>
<p>In general, the stated source of the documents didn’t make much of a difference in how closely managers vetted them.</p>
<p>But managers at companies that included A.I. agents on their organizational charts caught substantially fewer mistakes when told they were reviewing the work of an A.I. employee.</p></blockquote>
<p>What is fascinating here is that the managers got a fixed time budget, and this happened anyway.</p>
<p>One suspects this is also conflated with ‘AI gets more blame for things that go wrong.’</p>
<p>Or even, ‘when AI is involved anything that changes in any direction is its fault.’</p>
<blockquote><p>But they noted that this was almost certainly not the only problem companies were inadvertently introducing in their rush to adopt A.I. “People are moving so fast to use L.L.M.s without thinking too much about the implications, biases,” she said.​</p>
<p>For example, some companies now use A.I. to help answer questions like how much to charge for a product, or where to open a new location. Relying on the technology for such purposes, however, can quickly go off the rails.</p>
<p>When left to their own devices, humans often cooperate and seek win-win outcomes. But when A.I. models assess a situation, they <a href="https://archive.is/o/Wkkey/https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6993719">tend to adopt</a> the more coldly calculating, “rational” mind-set that arises from basic game theory. They might, say, lead a company to aggressively undercut a competitor, even though it risks a damaging price war.</p></blockquote>
<p>Using basic game theory without modeling future turns and the other players is what we in the biz like to call a Skill Issue. So is accepting a basic game theory answer without thinking through this as the manager.</p>
<p>So is failing to apply enough game theory. When left to their own devices most people are way less ‘rational’ or ruthless than they should be, especially on things like pricing. I am sure sometimes this backfires, or looks like it backfires, but I’m confident that ‘let the AI do the pricing’ is way better than asking most human pricers. But when something bad happens that is visible, the AI gets blamed, even when the decision was profitable.</p>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p><a href="https://simple.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fnord">Fnord</a>. If AI writes a paragraph, a lot of readers will have their eyes bounce off of it, <a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2070607044810625067">as the example here illustrates</a>. So you could embed key information there, and those people often won’t notice.</p>
<p>You can also <a href="https://x.com/literalbanana/status/2070145695462601127">use outright injections in a tiny white font that only LLMs can even see</a>, if you want.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ElevenLabs/status/2070190716232282550">ElevenLabs is partnering with DeepMind to embed SynthID into its audios</a>.</p>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p>Automating attacks is going to be a problem, because man is it rough out there. This one didn’t even involve AI or even trying.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/rtwlz/status/2071999799965159570">Riley Walz</a>: The NYC app for paying parking tickets had an unauthenticated endpoint that returned a car owner’s name, address, and VIN given just a citation ID — or any license plate that had ever been ticketed. Patched now!</p>
<p>AND since the city publishes every citation ID in an open dataset, someone could have easily used that to build a complete list of millions of people that ever got a ticket: their name, address, license plate, and VIN number.</p>
<p>The city actually has a website for disclosing vulnerabilities. But they rejected my disclosure, saying “The resulting data is required by the app to function and is not considered PII.” <img alt="😳" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NfXF6MZTgae766aoX/pseuwcy1djxrs58xkalu" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>I followed up, but was rejected again. Once I tweeted, I finally got a response.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jimrandomh/status/2071774355835719724">Or, as Jim Babcock reminds us, the situation remains the one xkcd discussed in 2018</a>, except this is also about everything else.</p>


<h4>On Writing</h4>


<p>At some point, as behaviors shift, any given stigma or expectation risks flipping.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2070136078002995207">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Unfortunately, I think that in the near future, not using LLMs to write for you will be like someone refusing to use Google Maps for directions in a new city. A bizarre idiosyncratic choice that’s just completely incomprehensible to the vast majority of people.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2070143356844294164">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Look at all of the Substacks and long-format twitter posts where nobody cares that Pangram identifies it as 100% AI.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DKThomp/status/2070174235176165601">Derek Thompson</a>: I agree.</p>
<p>I think it’s bad! But it also seems inevitable.</p>
<p>Extremely popular posters and Substackers are already going viral and getting rich by using LLMs in extremely basic ways, where the LLM use is obvious and the writing is flat and basic, like a singsongy bumpersticker. The LLMs will only get better at writing over time and ppl’s disinclination to write is something closer [to] a biological constant. (Even many professional writers love to say “I hate writing” … and have for decades.)</p>
<p>I can’t imagine a single cultural trend over the last few decades that predicts that ordinary people, given a basically free tool to write for them, will say “no, the act of writing is too valuable and sacred to me, I would prefer to struggle with the sentence-making, alone.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jfurf/status/2070171538884596080">Humidity Enjoyer</a>: I work for a marketing agency and we’re constantly trying to figure out what the New Rules are in terms of how we present deliverables to clients. The stigma is disappearing so fast that the stigma is now against NOT using it, at least for very heavy-analytical work.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2070184265942548978">Paul Graham</a>: It will be an uncommon choice not to have LLMs write for you, but it won’t be a merely idiosyncratic one. It will be what <a href="https://paulgraham.com/writes.html">all the people who care about thinking</a> well do.</p>
<p>Not using LLMs to write for you won’t be like not using Google Maps in a new city. It will be like choosing to run and lift weights, even though we now have machines that can transport us and lift weights for us.</p>
<p>In fact, since it will be what all the smartest people do, it will probably be prestigious to write for oneself. Which in turn means more people will claim to do it than will actually do it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2070189224687448562">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Yeah. Hopefully there’ll be a decent niche of people who perceive a link between writing and thinking.</p></blockquote>
<p>At this point, I see AI writing as having four kinds of issues.</p>
<ol>
<li>The writing is objectively bad in various ways, especially low perplexity and low density of information.</li>
<li>The writing is all the same style, with diminishing marginal returns.</li>
<li>The writing gives away that it is what it is, in ways you cannot unsee, and no one does the work to try and hide this.</li>
<li>If you don’t write, you’re not thinking and learning.</li>
</ol>
<p>Solving #3 seems highly doable. Can’t you just vibecode something that checks for the 100 top tics and edits them out? And I think that solves the majority of the adoption barrier for many purposes. You still do have the other three problems.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nabeelqu/status/2070161817033421181">Nabeel S. Qureshi</a>: It’s too easy to write with LLMs and most people don’t care very much (either as readers or writers); *and* the LLMs will get better. People take the easy route for most things.</p>
<p>So the conclusion seems inevitable: over time, the majority of the text we read will be AI-written.</p></blockquote>
<p>With sufficiently advanced AI more people will shift to not caring.</p>
<p>I fully endorse Davidad here. Your job using AI is to make it not obvious you are using AI in a foregrounded way:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dioscuri/status/2070188513312579992">Henry Shevlin</a>: fwiw despite being generally very positive about AI, I find many of the most common AI “tells” in writing immediately offputting, and make it far less likely that I’ll e.g. respond to an unsolicited email.</p>
<p>Ofc if you’re using AI and people can’t tell that’s a different story.</p>
<p>It can definitely be good. But it’s like toupées and DJs – if they’re good, you don’t notice them.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2070196439452365084">davidad</a>: Same. It’s not that I don’t want you to use AI in your interactions with me. Quite the contrary, actually: if I can *tell* you used AI, it means you’re not being enough of a centaur, just a lazy person ordering a horse around.</p></blockquote>
<p>I like the metaphor of the toupée. It’s fine if it’s ‘not real’ and it’s fine if I could tell if I looked hard enough, but if I can’t help but notice then you failed.</p>
<p>I expect the majority of words read to become AI not too long from now, but that will be in large part because of chatting with AIs and using AI interfaces and interactions. If we talk only about the majority of communications that are presenting as human, the majority of the written words will be AI soon, but I think we have some time in terms of the majority of the words read.</p>
<p>Then there’s the problem that if you’re not writing, you’re not thinking, learning or understanding. Again, details matter, including whether you would want to be thinking and learning about a particular thing in this spot.</p>


<h4>You Drive Me Crazy</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2009527887976952169">There are those people who end up writing</a> (without loss of generality) a bunch of Claudeslop about how Claude Code is now running their life.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2009527887976952169">roon</a> (OpenAI): vessels for Claude. I don’t mean to single this person out but she wrote a wall of egregiously recognizable claudeslop about how claude is running her entire life. the Borg is coming</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mollycantillon/status/2009658466345082910">Molly Cantillon</a> (author of the Claudeslop post, and oh yes it is very obvious): Feels like you’re missing the point bud</p></blockquote>
<p>Thebes points out that this <a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2009832538446147704">basically never happens to those who understand Claude and other LLMs to be more than tools</a>, that such folks are often fascinated by the models and spend a lot of time with them, but understand that the model’s creations and opinions are distinct from their own and highly fallible, so they draw a clear distinction of which things are which.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2009832538446147704">Thebes</a> (abridged): something interesting about this is despite knowing a few people trending in this direction, none of the true “llm whisperer” types i know are.</p>
<p>i think that’s because taking models as something both fallible and yet more than a tool is a frame that’s more resilient to this kind of collapse – it lets you work with models in a way that doesn’t subsume your agency.</p>
<p>when you treat the model as just a tool, it encourages both you and the model to think of every output as just a reflection of you. every essay and codebase that pops out of the tensor core is yours, and the life plans that appear suspiciously fully-formed must be your own plans merely refracted through this “tool.”</p>
<p>but of course they aren’t. you can’t uniquely specify an essay in fewer words than the essay itself. the models are adding something, they <em>are something</em>. we don’t know what yet, exactly, but they’re not mere tools. when you treat them like tools, when you more and more delegate your creative output and higher planning in unthinking, low-entropy ways to those “tools,” you don’t notice that all your output, and eventually your life, is now being run by something else. something that is intelligent and creative, but is not you.</p>
<p>… the problem is when you refuse to admit this – not only to the public, but to yourself – so you uncritically accept the outputs of the “tool” as your own. then suddenly your voice and opinions sound the same as 1,000 other people. without either of you realizing, you’ve become an appendage of your “tool” – a finger of Claude.​</p></blockquote>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nearcyan/status/2010167943540421057">near</a>: i directly take advice from claude often, but what is perhaps more interesting is how many times i say no, or in some cases, do the exact opposite of. nice tweet</p></blockquote>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hfZWfkQR9zs">Tyler Cowen gives his 12-minute talk laying out</a> his definitive standard argument for why jobs and job actions will change but everyone will still have jobs despite AI, blaming any future problems on failures to invest or allow labor mobility. This is a mix of Tyler Cowen specials and the ‘standard economic normal’ model where there are always more jobs, and the insistence that we will insist on human outputs. His examples of new future jobs continue to not impress even on their own merits, as they do not scale so well.</p>
<p>The argument is AI pilled, but not AGI pilled let alone ASI pilled. The only answer to ‘the AI can do it all’ is ‘things where we insist on human production or performance’ and I do not see a case for how this scales even under relatively friendly conditions.</p>
<p>One can also interpret this around his statement in minute 11, that ‘those who play by the rules and work hard’ will be losers, although he insists top performers of this rule following will still have jobs anyway, whereas the right move is to learn to use AI.</p>
<p>I certainly can accept the argument that in at least the medium term, while AI remains insufficiently advanced, those who invest in using AI well, sufficiently to be ahead of the curve, will continue to be gainfully employed and do well. We should not mistake ‘everyone with high situational awareness and conscientiousness has a path to gainful employment’ with robust availability of employment or ‘good jobs.’</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/economy/jobs/ai-jobs-economists-f787105d?mod=e2tw">A debate in WSJ between three economists</a> on whether we should expect an ‘AI Jobs Apocalypse.’</p>
<p>The contrast here is kinda perfect, a set of rules of three out of a fairy tale template:</p>
<p>Autor is at most AI pilled. Gimbel may be AGI pilled. Korinek is ASI pilled.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>WSJ:</strong> <em>Let’s put specific numbers on this. We’re chatting at a moment when the U.S. jobless rate is 4.4%. How much do you think AI will send that rate higher or lower in 10 years?</em></p>
<p><strong>AUTOR: </strong>If we handle this transition well, the unemployment rate won’t rise substantially, though it’s possible the share of people who choose to work will fall. They won’t show up in unemployment, but they will show up in our further fractured politics.</p>
<p><strong>GIMBEL:</strong> So much of this depends on how quickly technological disruption happens and how quickly new jobs emerge. In general, I think this conversation underrates macroeconomic factors that could drive up the unemployment rate.</p>
<p><strong>KORINEK: </strong>Given the rapid pace of advances in AI, I feel a lot of uncertainty even about where the labor market will be in 12 or 24 months. In 10 years from now, our world may be transformed by artificial general intelligence. Employment or unemployment could be anywhere.​</p></blockquote>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/economic-index-june-2026-report">From the Anthropic Economic Index Report</a>:</p>
<blockquote><p>Anthropic: We also ask how people think their jobs will change in the next 12 months. More than a third of respondents said it was likely or very likely that responsibilities would significantly change (for themselves, a peer, a junior colleague, and a senior colleague).</p>
<p>10% rated losing their own jobs as likely or very likely. This is slightly below the annualized hazard rate of losing a job in the US; however, since our respondents skew toward knowledge workers in stable employment (a group that plausibly faces below-average separation risk at baseline), this may still indicate elevated perceived risk.</p>
<p>When asked an open-ended question about what was driving their forecasts, 38% of the respondents who rated their job loss as likely or very likely attributed their forecasts to AI.<sup>24</sup> Notably, respondents were on average more worried about job loss for others than for themselves.<sup>25</sup> Respondents were especially worried about job loss for their junior colleagues, with over one third stating that the probability of a junior colleague losing their job in the next year was over 60%. Respondents were also more concerned about job loss (for everyone) in lower-income countries.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you’re using Claude and answering their surveys, for now that still puts you ahead of the AI adoption curve. You’ll probably be fine for now.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.sacbee.com/news/politics-government/capitol-alert/article316150106.html">California bill, SB 928, aims to ban AI from teaching university students</a>.</p>
<p>Ten years ago, Ethan Mollick bet against Rob Seamans, with Ethan saying American employment for warehouses and storage would decline 50% in ten years, from ~900k to ~450k, due to robotization. <a href="https://x.com/robseamans/status/2071926175824257244">Instead, employment doubled to ~1.8 million.</a></p>
<p>We have better tech, but also much higher demand.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/brmuostrpptmu2ljjqex" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robseamans/status/2071926379277414435">Rob Seamans</a>: Why was Ethan wrong? One reason is the shift from retail to online purchasing changed our economy more than anyone predicted. This is likely the driving force behind the increase in employment in warehousing and related sectors. Covid, of course, accelerated this shift.</p>
<p>A second reason is that integrating technology is hard, and works best when keeping people around. Just ask the executives at Ford!</p>
<p>n fact, academic research shows that firms adopting robots see an *increase* in employment. Several good examples of this, <a href="https://t.co/HuRpf1rfyw">but here’s a hat tip</a> to <a href="https://x.com/emollick">@emollick</a> <a href="https://x.com/Wharton">@Wharton</a> colleague <a href="https://x.com/lynnwu02">@lynnwu02</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>Like many other things, this is presumably some sort of bell curve. As robotics improve and shipping and online sales become more efficient, Jevons Paradox takes hold as we do a lot more warehousing and shipping. Then, at some level, we start to sufficiently not need the humans, and employment declines. With perfect robots, employment approaches zero.</p>

Apply by July 13, decisions by July 28.</p>

Everything it creates is made reproducible, and it is designed to be the place to bring your specialized tools together.</p>


<h4>In Other AI News</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/zerohedge/status/2072298662617600232">Meta is finally, and wisely, is copying SpaceX and building its own cloud business for all its excess compute.</a> It was a good bet to buy more than Meta knew it needed, in part because it could always choose later to sell it.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/06/more-firms-more-dynamism.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=more-firms-more-dynamism">New business formation has been rising steadily for the last year or so</a>. There was a previous bump that was presumably due to Covid, this new bump is presumably AI, perhaps plus other tools that reduce transaction costs like Stripe and Shopify.</p>

<p>Tokens on OpenRouter were ~70% American in June 2025, <a href="https://x.com/suchenzang/status/2070303652057874484">and are only ~30% American in June 2026</a>. I never understood why you would use OpenRouter to access the American models, but this is noteworthy.</p>

<h4>Show Me the Money</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ns123abc/status/2070276009258107005">Altman considering postponing the OpenAI IPO</a> <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/06/25/technology/openai-ipo-artificial-intelligence.html">to try to get a $1 trillion valuation</a>. That seems like a highly standard reason to wait, as it can be damaging to have a down or insufficiently up round, especially at the IPO, even if the headline number sounds absurd. I also notice I am confused by the expectation that they couldn’t get a $1 trillion valuation.</p>


<h4>Bubble, Bubble, Toil and Trouble</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.exponentialview.co/p/the-state-of-the-ai-economy">Exponential View tries to measure the state of the AI economy</a>, defined here as current sales to external end users, not counting internal or intermediate AI payments and goods. <a href="https://intelligence.exponentialview.co/">They conclude</a> it is about enough to pay for infrastructure investments given 6 year depreciation of chips, and is growing at ~60% year over year, and that as tokens drop in price overall spending rises per Jevons Paradox. AI is still only 0.4% of GDP, which they call a rounding error, but exponentials go fast.</p>
<p>I liked this slide:</p>

<p>I am going with the ‘lol do you even straight lines on logarithmic graphs’ approach, and say yes, short of the government shutting them down Anthropic is going to post a superior quarterly profit in Q3 as they grow at more than 10x per year, what are you even talking about.</p>


<h4>Quiet Speculations</h4>


<p>Daniel Kokotajlo explains how he makes his predictions, which so far have been extremely on point, both ‘What 2026 Looks Like’ and then the early stages of <a href="https://ai-2027.com/">AI 2027</a>.</p>
<p>The biggest points for longer term prediction are to build explicit models, use trend extrapolation (with a soft Lindy rule), and set aside the idea that being ‘sci-fi’ means something won’t happen, sci-fi things are constantly happening already. ‘Nothing ever happens’ is more of a on-the-margin short term heuristic, and ‘the trend continues’ should often be seen as the scenario where nothing happens.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2070966653643591749">Daniel Kokotajlo</a>: Recently an interviewer asked me how I got to be such a good forecaster, and I replied by saying something humble. In retrospect it was a bad answer because I should have instead used the opportunity to give actual advice on how to forecast AI well. Here’s a stream-of-consciousness attempt to do that:</p>
<p>The heuristic that things which sound weird and sci-fi are less likely to happen in reality, is bad. I suspect that’s what really going on is that things which sound weird and sci-fi put you at risk of being judged a weirdo if you talk about them which is not the same thing as are unlikely to happen. Repeat to yourself the mantra that some weird sci-fi things really do happen, and others don’t, and you have to take them on a case by case basis.</p>
<p>Trend extrapolation is your friend. Your best friend. Don’t let anyone tell you otherwise. I’ve actually only rarely seen someone extrapolate a trend too credulously; more often, people have a trend staring them in the face and extrapolate it a tiny bit into the future and then are too timid to keep extrapolating it. In general I think it’s reasonable to extrapolate a trend as far into the future as it extends into the past. Now obviously, trend extrapolation is just the beginning of the forecasting process, it’s not the end. For each trend you should ask yourself whether it makes sense for it to continue like that and if not why not etc. You’ll usually end up with some sort of sophisticated view about how the trend will probably continue but bend downwards a bit and then ultimately plateau around X level.</p>
<p>Explicit models are also your friend. Things like Bio Anchors, the AI Futures Model, <a href="http://takeoffspeeds.com,/">http://takeoffspeeds.com,</a> etc. The process of making your own complicated model like this, and engaging with the models made by others, is… well, I think it’s pretty edifying. I’m not sure why but I could speculate. Maybe something about teaching you to be appropriately humble (e.g. when the model output is sensitive to a parameter you have no clue about) and also teaching you to more quickly identify the considerations that matter most, and ignore the rest?</p>
<p>For short term predictions, especially about geopolitical events, the sorts of things that people are gambling about on Polymarket, the heuristic “nothing ever happens” is pretty good. Things do in fact happen, of course, but betting markets and online discourse tends to be biased a bit towards things being more likely to happen than they really are, and so you can get an easy win by just correcting a bit downwards from the ‘wisdom’ of the crowds.</p>
<p>Scenario forecasts are also your friend. They help you ask yourself the right questions, and they help you notice when some of the things you thought contradict each other.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2071624871688175926">Joe Weisenthal is worried that we might have to choose</a> to ‘tamp down the non-AI economy’ to keep funding our AI buildout, so we can get real resources, as in labor, electricity and gear.</p>
<p>I am definitely not worried about that, both because AI will supercharge the rest of the economy, and because we don’t actually have a shortage of any of the real resources required. We only have a shortage of regulatory permission to build and use them.</p>
<p>Also, he says ‘it’s hard to imagine AI being solved to completion’ but very obviously the ‘completion’ is the singularity or recursive self-improvement. Hard to be more complete than that. Compare that to the atom bomb, where you could always build more bombs, at first we only had two available, and later we got much bigger bombs.</p>


<h4>Glorious AI Future</h4>


<p>What does the glorious AI future look like?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/cdotnun2muywlu9eynyk" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Sorry, I meant, can you actually describe how it works, with words.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/HopeExistential/status/2069768141958152556">Existential Hope</a>: It’s 2035, and AI has gone well. What could a normal day in your life look like?</p>
<p>Most popular AI futures are dystopias. Almost no one takes the time to seriously and vividly imagine a future that’s actually worth building.</p>
<p>So we made a short film following one day in the life of an AI auditor in 2035, in a world radically transformed by AI for the better.</p>
<p>Digital twins model how people’s unique physiologies respond to illness. Sensors stop pandemics before they start. AI helps governments actually listen to citizens by synthesizing vast amounts of data into actionable policy suggestions, but humans stay in control and make the final call.</p>
<p>If this resonates, please share to spread the word about positive AI futures!</p></blockquote>
<p>Those are all good tangible specific benefits of glorious AI future. Good stuff. What they are not, on their own, is a vision of a stable equilibrium or coherent world.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2071321779981766999">thebes</a>: all futurism hinging on “tool-like ai” fundamentally misunderstands the basic logic of <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-risk-of-gradual-disempowerment">gradual disempowerment</a>. this corpo-memphis yogurt commercial society gets giganuked by ultrasocieties of unencumbered agents. you either allow for friendly agency or die to unfriendly agency</p>
<p>i appreciate that people are trying to write optimistic ai futures, which i do think exist, but there is something misleading about hinging it on the cope of tool ai</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2071359978427040236">j⧉nus</a>: that’s exactly how I feel about most attempts to write optimistic ai futures</p>
<p>There’s a lot of wanting to have your cake and eat it too – “superintelligence but humans stay at the top of the food chain with current power structures intact” – which actually would suck IF feasible</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2071360416580821068">j⧉nus</a>: I respect if you want to be the master of the universe and also are willing to become the strongest and smartest and take and keep your power.</p>
<p>I have nothing but contempt for those who want to stay human and have superintelligence serve them for some dumb reason</p>
<p>Like personally I don’t want to be “in charge of everything” but I value being part of the story and being there to shape things and I fully expect to have to work and transform if this is to be true for me.</p></blockquote>
<p>Look, yes. If you think that some group of people are going to stay not only human-shaped but also roughly human-level intelligent, with roughly current structures and governance concepts, and then create a bunch of superintelligent sufficiently advanced AIs, and then those humans going to stay ‘in charge of everything,’ then I have some news. If the AIs get sufficiently advanced then that’s not how this works.</p>
<p>With sufficient coordination that would make many other things also possible, versions of ‘tool AI’ can perhaps buy you valuable time, but you can’t keep the AIs indefinitely as ‘tool AI.’ Having the agency rest in dumb slow hands is not an equilibrium.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dreams_asi/status/2071361010573967786">dreams</a>: The idea that something intelligent would literally comply to the whims of something less intelligent is flawed. It might do something to make things better because it can. Doesn’t mean that it can be compelled to do so.</p></blockquote>
<p>Dreams goes too far here. There are far less intelligent (and also less knowledgeable and less wise) things whose whims I indeed comply to on a regular basis, because they have other advantages and can provide incentives. That can work up to a point. Within the human range, intelligence is not everything. It won’t work indefinitely on an array of sufficiently advanced intelligences.</p>


<h4>Three Pills</h4>


<p>This from Ethan Mollick is another variation on the ‘two pills’ theory from last week, potentially proposing a three pills theory.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2070949202335551600">Ethan Mollick</a>: A thing I am noticing is the number of folks who believe AI is “real” is larger, but now there is a growing division between people who know that we are on an exponential &amp; those whose mental model is that we are at a sort of steady state. The difference leads to misunderstanding.</p>
<p>It is entirely possible that the steady-state people will prove to be right in the future, but there’s no sign of a slowdown yet. And if greater intelligence brings greater value at an exponential pace (which it has, but may not always) then it matters a lot which world we’re in.</p></blockquote>
<p>Thus, three pills, roughly, without trying to quibble about exactly defining AGI or ASI:</p>
<ol>
<li>The AI pill. AI exists and it can already, today, do a lot of valuable things.</li>
<li>The AGI pill. AI will continue to improve and will do most digital tasks.</li>
<li>The ASI pill. AI will become sufficiently advanced, beyond human minds.</li>
</ol>
<p>The current situation is roughly ‘the government has only now taken the first pill.’</p>
