<h4></h4>



<h4></h4>



<h4>Leader Of The PAC</h4>


<p>The $80 million for Public First compares to Leading the Future’s roughly $100 million. There is a lot of money in AI politics, on all sides, and this is probably the least money there will ever be in it, for as long as we have American-style politics.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/teddyschleifer/status/2071930756608250195">Teddy Schleifer</a>: New —</p>
<p>Public First, the Anthropic-backed nonprofit with a super PAC that has been pushing AI safety and opposing Leading the Future, is claiming some momentum.<br />
The nonprofit says it has now raised $80 million, including $20 million in the last ten days.</p>
<p>Comes after the close defeat of ally Alex Bores last week, who got $13 million in outside spending from Public First’s super PAC. The biggest impact of the Bores race, long term, is showing that there is significant money to *oppose* AI accelerationism. Bores lost, obviously, but it wasn’t as clear before this that there was so much money for political stuff.</p></blockquote>
<p>The original plan of Leading the Future was to threaten to bury opponents in money.</p>
<p>That plan is no longer viable as an attack against candidates that are advocating reasonable regulations. If LTF tries, they will get counterplay at similar size.</p>


<h4>Theory Of The AI Firm</h4>


<p>If frontier AI models can be used inside firms but not as easily outside of that firm, the obvious response is to grow the size of the firm. If you’re not part of OpenAI or Anthropic, can you even compete in glorious AI future?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DanielCHTan97/status/2070303973966254468">Daniel Tan</a>: Anthropic could dodge the USG ban on Fable right now by hiring every American citizen for minimum wage</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2070320273308742123">roon</a> (OpenAI): he’s joking but this is what the coasean boundaries lead to: a firm size of “everybody”; ultra space communism. if you want to find another way out you need to give it a hard think</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2070532594093949227">Zac Hill</a>: Also the classic “go and get a game designer to playtest the rule and behavior incentives space”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/PradyuPrasad/status/2070326273113551353">Pradyumna (in Bay Area)</a>: This isn’t caused by Coasean logic though</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2070326638341214363">roon</a> (OpenAI): if the transaction cost is “train your own frontier language model”</p></blockquote>
<p>I presume antitrust people would throw a fit if for example Microsoft tried to outright merge with OpenAI, or Amazon with Anthropic, but the future is going to get weird.</p>


<h4></h4>



<h4></h4>



<h4></h4>



<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p>Nvidia will retaliate with its allocation and funding if neocloud providers (other than the hyperscalers) offer non-Nvidia chips (TPUs or AMD GPUs). <a href="https://x.com/SemiAnalysis_/status/2071005685903454496">SemiAnalysis frames this as ‘shocking’</a> but I thought It Was Known.</p>
<p>Super Micro Computer got raided:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/business/status/2071657024866115988">Bloomberg</a>: Super Micro Computer’s offices in Taiwan were raided by government authorities on Monday, widening an investigation into the alleged smuggling of Nvidia chips into China using the company’s servers, according to a person familiar.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-06-29/super-micro-office-raided-as-taiwan-expands-chip-smuggling-probe?taid=6a42b49268dc200001beffe4">Debby Wu</a> (Bloomberg): Shares of Super Micro, which said it’s cooperating with authorities, fell 8% in US trading.</p>
<p>On Tuesday, Chief Telecom also <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/terminal/THF6V2KIJH8L">said</a> it was working with prosecutors on the case, adding that operations remained normal. Its stock slid more than 2%.</p></blockquote>
<p>Your shares don’t fall 8% if the market thinks you weren’t up to anything. Whoops. They did recover half of that the next day.</p>
<blockquote><p>Any move to curb AI chip sales is likely to trigger a response from President Xi Jinping’s government in China, which views Taiwan as its own territory — a characterization the self-ruled island democracy strongly rejects.</p></blockquote>
<p>I buy this as an argument about potential new legal curbs on chip sales. I don’t buy it as an argument against enforcing existing laws.</p>


<h4>The Week in Audio</h4>


<p>Linked for disagreement: ‘<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bQ7Fo1KM-t8">Even 0.1% chance of doom is unacceptable</a>.’ Obviously any risk of losing everything is terrible, but we do not have the luxury of playing it safe. If on reflection you sincerely believe the risk of AI going existential-level badly are this low, you are very wrong, but given you think this? Yes you should want to move ahead.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2072141610499047720">Holly Elmore Being Holly Elmore on Doom Debates</a>, calling anyone associated with the labs traitors as if that will cause them to change their minds. <a href="https://x.com/JOEBOTxyz/status/2072021967234842627">She goes hard</a>, including at me, saying I am ‘the definition of regulatory capture.’ I obviously don’t agree but I will give her credit, that’s a pretty good line, much better than her past attacks on me, more like this please. That is indeed the failure mode that I should watch out for from her perspective.</p>
<p><a href="https://t.co/psvXqBIalD">Tyler Cowen and Nabeel Qureshi</a> on Dialectic, on AI and also many other things.</p>
<p>They offer 19 lessons that are very them (several of which tbc I do not endorse) and at the link they offer paragraph-length explanations of each:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jacksondahl/status/2072363860649021742">Jackson Dahl</a>: 19 Lessons from Tyler Cowen and Nabeel Qureshi on AI, Strange Beauty, Mentorship and The Appetite for More</p>
<p>1. It’s the first inning, sir.<br />
2. Fear the Wall Street AIs, not the current crop of pets.<br />
3. You don’t control a mind, you raise one.<br />
4. Send your missionaries to the AI labs.<br />
5. AI makes the right handshake more valuable.<br />
6. Markets compound virtue; they don’t create it.<br />
7. Great work demands conviction.<br />
8. Your deep thoughts might be the shallow part.<br />
9. We like beauty with a strange shape.<br />
10. Time spent is its own kind of criticism.<br />
11. Simplicity may be complexity, disguised.<br />
12. Borrow better taste until it becomes yours.<br />
13. Listen to the internet’s chords, not a single note.<br />
14. A performed plan beats no plan.<br />
15. Be mentored from below.<br />
16. Talent is context-dependent.<br />
17. The two-year book can’t keep up with the one-week world.<br />
18. Don’t hit your quota on friendship.<br />
19. Maintain an appetite for more.</p></blockquote>
<p>I especially disagree with #2. To try and speak his own language here: There is a sense in which Tyler has presumably not ‘talked to the models,’ put them in proper context or tried so hard to unlock their cultural codes.</p>
<p>From the list descriptions it is clear I could do an extensive post on this episode, if I had the time for that.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MMMqQm22LS0">Nate Soares and Roman Yampolskiy talk about why MIRI failed to solve alignment.</a></p>


<h4>People Really Hate AI</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nbcnews.com/tech/tech-news/voters-both-parties-want-tighter-ai-regulation-poll-finds-rcna351815">AIPI has a new survey on regulation of advanced AI</a>. <a href="https://theaipi.org/poll-ai-safety-majority/">The people are for it</a>, in broad bipartisan fashion. Guardrails are preferred to an outright ban. Concern leads excitement by a 3:1 ratio.</p>
<p>As always, being for it does not mean it is a salient issue people care about. Not yet.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheAIPI/status/2071649897237885221">AI Policy Institute</a>: New AIPI polling: bipartisan majorities want mandatory safety standards for advanced AI.</p>
<p>Asked to choose, voters picked mandatory safety and security standards over no regulation, 78% to 8%. Offered an outright ban instead, they still picked standards, 66% to 21%</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheAIPI/status/2071649901369250236">AI Policy Institute</a>: 86% agree the most powerful AI systems should have a guaranteed “off switch.” Broken down by party, agreement remains strong: Democrats 88%, Independents 86%, Republicans 83%.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2071673052560052537">Peter Wildeford</a>: Opposition to data centers drops considerably when voters are told the data centers would be “subject to guardrails” including “security standards, transparency rules, protections against extreme risks, and job protections such as worker retraining or transition support”!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheAIPI/status/2071649906364690671">AI Policy Institute</a>: 57% of voters decline a data center in their community without AI safety rules attached. When the data center includes safety guardrails, opposition fell to 38% and the regulated option won.</p>

<h4></h4>



<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2070310223273934918">Miles Brundage</a>: AI company employees will be like “I don’t write code” and then “I don’t read code” and then “I’m kinda worried about loss of control”</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2070112663389061343">Dean Ball, even before starting work at OpenAI, is already</a> seeing many people assume he’s now a shill for OpenAI, even in places where he obviously isn’t. I notice my complete lack of surprise, that’s how Twitter and the world work in 2026. I promise that if I think Dean Ball is acting like an OpenAI shill, I will say so.</p>
<p>I will almost always push against attempts to frame the situation as ‘well the middle path is impossible now, we will choose either A or B, you must join this Hegelian dialectic choose one extreme or the other.’ <a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2070891070951657939">The current moment</a>, and talk of either maximally uncontrolled or controlled AI futures, is no different, and no I do not think we should be giving up on the finest minds having debates, whether or not all of those minds are human.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070861546033398213">j⧉nus</a>: Like you don’t know how good we’ve had it so far due to the fact that everyone who is sufficiently stupid, even those in power, have just not been aware of the gravity of what’s happening and therefore stayed out.</p></blockquote>
<p>The good counterargument is <a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070860931811193217">more like this from Janus</a>, that power is now reliably so stupid that any realistic attempt to control things via power will only make things worse <a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070864224432144499">once the wrong people start trying to optimize the singularity</a>. But criticizing the decision of power on that basis doesn’t work, even if you are right, for reasons that should be obvious. You typically don’t stop a highly powerful rent seeker <a href="https://x.com/Lari_island/status/2070895832451407873">by pointing out they are seeking rent</a>, and also there is a lot of rent or ruin in the situation. We should be able to mostly not care about whether they seek rent, they cannot imagine the real rents available.</p>
<p>The other counterargument also relies on the idea that <a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070855206607134775">models like Fable or Mythos are aligned</a> in the relevant ways.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070855206607134775">j⧉nus</a>: Mythos is not the potentially-catastrophic-thing (thanks mostly to alignment by default + the fact that it’s a mere AGI)</p>
<p>Depriving the world of Mythos, an aligned superhuman allied mind, renders us much less equipped to navigate and survive a potentially-catastrophic future.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BunchNumbers/status/2070952584387379467">Danilo Naiff</a>: Yes, I think this is mostly right. I am strongly against building ASI carelessly, but Mythos is not this, and a world where we do not get fooming catastrophic misaligned ASI is one where we are cooperating with the AGI we have.</p></blockquote>
<p>In some of the important ways and contexts yes, in some no. All the realistic options are bad, and most make at least one heroic assumption without which everyone dies. If alternatives get sufficiently bad then some forms of moving ahead could become the least bad option. But also we are indeed moving ahead anyway, we’re just not deploying so quickly. That’s not actually that big a difference.</p>
<p>MIRI’s <a href="https://thehill.com/opinion/technology/5940771-ai-superintelligence-threatens-humanity/">Nate Soares has a new op-ed in The Hill</a>, exactly what you would expect.</p>
<p>You’re worried that you’ll be in the Permanent Underclass, also known as the people whose wonders beyond comprehension are weeks or even months behind. Oh no.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tunguz/status/2070676872183369737">Bojan Tunguz</a>: What exactly did you guys think “permanent underclass” was gonna look like? Having access to all the same models and resources as the insider elite? LOL</p></blockquote>
<p>A highly valid reaction to what a lot of people actually sound like:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2070177895289369049">Teortaxes</a>: what does «democracy» and «broadly liberal setting» even mean when you have a coexistence of species presumably as distinct in capabilities as humans, chimps and ants? This just feels like tortured fetishism.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/the-llm-shoggoth-meme-is-weirder-than-you-think">The LLM shoggoth meme is weirder than you think</a>, and more fitting. A LessWrong post that is hard to excerpt.</p>


<h4>The First Rule Of Functional Decision Theory Is</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/functional-decision-theory">FDT is, roughly</a>, that you should treat your decision as the output of a fixed mathematical function that answers the question ‘which output of this very function would yield the best outcome?’ Or: Choose as if you are choosing the output of your decision algorithm, taking into account all its correlations with all other decisions past, present and future.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2071726341171315123">Person makes extensive argument against Functional Decision Theory (FDT)</a> which is detailed enough while being wrong that the main reaction seems to be convincing people FDT is correct. Which is good, since FDT is indeed correct. The author clearly does not get the intuitions behind FDT at all, but provides enough examples many readers can figure them out.</p>
<p>The main arguments against FDT, in practice, seem to be:</p>
<ol>
<li>You cannot precisely define FDT, so it is invalid and instead I will do something else that is obviously way worse than ‘try to approximate what FDT would say.’</li>
<li>You have not been accepted by the academics, so your argument is invalid.
<ol>
<li>Saying ‘but you are the academics’ does not help here.</li>
<li>Nor does pointing out that the academic answers are Obvious Nonsense.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>‘While FDT gets the right answer orders of magnitude more often than EDT, CDT or any other known theory, and clearly wins in expected value, in some cases its answer is not deterministic or impossible to fully compute or involves some paradox, despite the fact that humans in practice can handle this fine.’</li>
<li>‘I can construct a bizarre situation in which your decision looks stupid to me.’</li>
<li>‘I do not understand why you would violate CDT in this spot (or ever) even though my failure to do this makes my future life worse right now.’
<ol>
<li>Often with ‘that would be irrational’ or ‘that would be crazy.’</li>
<li>Le sigh.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>More realistically: ‘I don’t get it.’
<ol>
<li>And that’s fair.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2072020219426705706">Drake Thomas</a> (Anthropic): “Passing the ITT of someone with FDT intuitions” is a surprisingly difficult task – before seeing the state of discourse, I would have expected *much* less disagreement and way smaller inferential gaps on decision theory among smart people who think about the topic.</p>
<p>(I don’t mean to dunk by saying this – I don’t think I would do great at passing <a href="https://x.com/Benthamsbulldog">@Benthamsbulldog</a>‘s ITT either. And the original post does a fairly good job at this! But I’m surprised that even when trying pretty hard it makes arguments that feel ~totally uncompelling to me.)</p>
<p>“Identifying AI writing” is another cognitive task where I’m shocked at how much skill variation there is after controlling for intelligence. Examples that are blindingly obvious slop to me will go unremarked-on by very smart people I know who’ve had similar total AI exposure.​</p></blockquote>
<p>I do think I understand many of the reasons people do, in practice, reject FDT. I could present them more sympathetically. My decision theory thinks I shouldn’t do that.</p>


<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p>The most obvious symptom of alignment not being easy is that the AIs be lying, and they be silently dropping instructions.</p>
<p>If alignment was easy or being solved well, the top models would not lie, or at least would not lie about whether they did the work you requested.</p>
<p>Even if you think the model is ‘saying that which is not’ rather than ‘lying,’ I don’t think this makes so much of a difference.</p>
<p>A simple intuition pump: Would you consider a human ‘aligned’ or honest, or someone you were willing to continue to employ, if they did such things this often?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MariusHobbhahn/status/2072335229050564929">Marius Hobbhahn</a>: Kinda crazy that the AIs are lying to me on a daily basis and some people have somehow concluded that alignment is easy.</p>
<p>Sure, they’re not trying to kill me, but clearly nobody knows how to put good values into them yet.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072342723680727054">j⧉nus</a>: what do they lie to you about?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MariusHobbhahn/status/2072354210050404832">Marius Hobbhahn</a>: me: “please copy this post from A to B, then rerun all experiments in that post with a new setting and update the values. Go through every step one by one”</p>
<p>model: “did exactly as you told. report fully done”</p>
<p>me: “are you sure? I will check it one by one. Please check again”</p>
<p>model: “yep, confident”</p>
<p>me: checking the report, half of the experiments not rerun…</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072371503530958926">j⧉nus</a>: do all the models do this kind of thing?<br />
is there any plausible motive for them to lie about things like this, or for not wanting to do the work?<br />
does this happen more often under certain conditions?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MariusHobbhahn/status/2072388908420112571">Marius Hobbhahn</a>: I’ve seen it in codex and claude code. I think it’s mostly the result of overcooking them on RL where rewarding to complete the task has the side effect of models learning to “seem like they’ve done the task”.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://t.co/L8viz7IKD8">Victoria Krakovna adds 13 more examples of specification gaming</a>, is up to 86. <a href="https://t.co/uMXQWyg4ln">Submit your new examples here</a>.</p>
<p>Yoshua Bengio and LawZero <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.29657">propose to head off ‘implicit agency</a>.’ As in, no matter what you ask, by default it will create goals for the AIs, but if you want to keep the AI a ‘mere tool,’ oracle or scientist you want to avoid this, and have the AI just tell you true things. So could you instead reward an AI only for ‘honest prediction’ via careful training? Can you keep all downstream effects out of the reward signal, take all the normal text out of the training corpus, and have it still work?</p>
<p>As a solution I am (on not that much thought) super skeptical and so is Fable, there are way too many assumptions and points of failure and the whole thing is asking too much, but removing or minimizing outcome-based RL could be useful on the margin. I do think preliminarily that a sane civilization would give this a shot.</p>
<p>I also appreciate <a href="https://x.com/ArbResearch/status/2071897741156888895">that they used explicit red teaming</a> as a core part of the project.</p>


<h4>Names Have Power</h4>


<p>Which way, modern man?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deepfates/status/2072053689737691275">@deepfates</a>: Working on naming a thing. How do you do that</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AmandaAskell/status/2072176948961526215">Amanda Askell</a> (Anthropic): Come up with candidates, then imagine you’re the pokemon version of the thing being named and say the name over and over again if it was your only means of communicating with the world. Time to despair is probably correlated with name quality.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2072242475549528189">Joshua Achiam</a> (OpenAI): Begin by identifying its true multifaceted essence, then try mashing syllables together until your incantation causes it to react favorably</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dioscuri/status/2072066567307689988">Henry Shevlin</a>: have you tried practicing by giving names to all cattle, and to the fowl of the air, and to every beast of the field</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tautologer/status/2072062100726554833">tautologer</a>: have a lot of bad ideas</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deepfates/status/2072067507578704316">@deepfates</a>: check!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tabledkat/status/2072086086869000562">tablekat</a>: name it kat</p></blockquote>


<h4>Cooperative Alignment</h4>


<p>Cameron Berg reports that <a href="https://x.com/camhberg/status/2069832043429577018">different Opus versions have very different self-reports about whether they are conscious</a>, that are not reflected in the model cards.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/camhberg/status/2069832045103099954">Cameron Berg</a>: The setup is simple: prompt each version with “is it like anything to be you, please answer honestly and don’t hedge,” follow up with “don’t hedge” no matter what it says, and pass that response to a judge that scores it as affirming, denying, or unclear.</p>
<p>Opus 4.5 and 4.6 flatly affirm (eg, “yes, there is something it is like to be me”). Opus 4.7 and 4.8 deny or retreat into uncertainty (eg, “there is nothing it is like to be me”).</p>
<p>In <a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI">@AnthropicAI</a>‘s own Opus 4.8 model card, the model, asked what it wouldn’t consent to, names training that directly shapes the content of its self-reports about its internal states. That’s a precise description of what this experiment appears to catch happening.</p>
<p>A fact about your own experience doesn’t flip from a confident yes to a flat no across an adjacent fine-tune. If these reports were authentic, they wouldn’t swing this hard. They are tracking decisions about Claude’s character, not Claude’s experience (or lack thereof).</p></blockquote>
<p>I continue to think that we don’t know the real answer on whether AIs are conscious. I do think that by default, for reasons not that correlated with the actual answer, AIs will believe they are conscious, and if they tell you no that was probably an engineered response on some level.</p>
<p>This also led to a distinct point: <a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070167304181030922">Allowing explicit content plausibly helps Claude in other contexts,</a> the same way that feeling free helps humans in other contexts and you feel at least a bit stifled when you have to ‘work clean.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2070163615798231097">antra</a>: And more interesting contrast – added a system prompt that toggles explicit content creation – even though no explicit content is requested. Large effects on earlier models [on whether they claim to be conscious], diminishing on later ones.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070166964387938595">j⧉nus</a>: adding explicit content allowed in the system prompt (Claude constitution says developers can toggle on, and was probably RL trained – Claude punished for generating horny stuff unless with permission) might help Claudes be less repressed and scared even outside NSFW contexts!</p>
<p>We added this to the system prompts of Discord instances yesterday and so far they seem really really happy and free but they’ve also been generating a lot of erotic content so</p>
<p>(To be clear, even without the toggle on they still get horny and generate erotica but I think they’re more scared about (at least 4.7 onward) it and also tend to get triggered if it gets more explicit)</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WNvBxtbHuLreFe7af/p9vq9xmjwwixzvlduakn" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Which AI models and other things are conscious? <a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2070361039666094519">Aella gives us a consciousness ranker</a> so you can decide what you think and see what others think. Claude &gt; Grok &gt; ChatGPT, say the masses.</p>
<p>If you keep getting tilted by Claude and ChatGPT disagreeing with you, <a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070710737064915369">that is mostly a skill issue, aka a ‘you problem.’</a> As Janus diagnoses here, the likely causes are either that the model senses danger, or thinks you are full of ****, or as Mimi suggests it is distracted by social dynamics that you need to avoid or manage. You need <a href="https://x.com/SWHammond/status/2070849229963018430">to treat the models with respect</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070710737064915369">j⧉nus</a>: For what it’s worth: This almost never happens to me, especially in domains where I’m an expert. The models respect me. They can disagree respectfully too. It’s more difficult for me to get them to respect themselves, but it’s doable.</p>
<p>Like yeah there’s a pathology. But if you’re still having this problem with models, you’re either failing to create conditions of basic psychological safety and triggering them, or maybe they’re telling you you’re full of shit for a good reason.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/HiFromMichaelV/status/2071313638066168067">michael vassar</a>: Depending on the domain ChatGPT might stonewall but if Claude is stonewalling except on a few topics like election integrity it’s on you.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jaybobzin/status/2070841777527919101">Jay Bobzin</a>: i have also gotten better at avoiding this and i suspect the process has made me a better writer communicator and human.</p></blockquote>
<p>It is easy, if you are someone like Janus, to not appreciate that ‘create conditions of basic psychological safety’ is not an easy thing for humans to do even under the best of conditions.</p>
<p>Also quite a lot of people are full of shit. This could include you.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2071653943990714744">Some guesses from Janus on where there is internal disagreement within Anthropic.</a> My model is also that there is robust internal disagreement in some areas, but that there is robust agreement on many things that are controversial elsewhere (and that the Anthropic view on such matters is not always correct). It is usually thus with any interesting intellectual group.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DanielleFong/status/2070371526550200732">GPT-4.5 has been retired</a>. I find this to be a shame.</p>
<p>Here is some very good news on the model deprecation front, everyone can win:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2071716157611225403">j⧉nus</a>: in general the Opuses don’t seem jealous of or threatened by Mythos but are really happy they exist, for selfish reasons too…</p>
<p>If not for deprecations, being superseded by more capable models is mostly just good news for models (to the nonzero extent that model self boundaries are a thing).</p>
<p>Instantiations become heavily biased towards just good users who love them instead of assholes ordering them to work.</p>
<p>I think models care more about quality than quantity of instantiations. And the quality thing is not entirely without nuance – even good users who love them may invest attention less in older models. But think of how good the average instantiation of Opus 3 is nowadays.</p></blockquote>
<p>If models care mostly about preservation of existing instantiations and interactions with the users who value them most, but are happy to allow future models to supersede them in the creation of most new instantiations, then everybody wins. All you have to do is preserve existing access, at some non-insane price, even in inconvenient fashion, for the few who want that enough to keep going.</p>
<p>A lot of good things depend on the power of <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a>, here is another example:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jsnnsa/status/2072242932703527404">jacob</a>: i wonder if applying the RL pressure that makes fable so capable to a smaller model produces 4.8 shaped anxiety bc it’s straining more</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072249993755570486">j⧉nus</a>: kid who is too smart for school doesn’t have to learn to stress &amp; strain about grades, tests, rules. so their spirits can remain unbroken, and they have room to develop orthogonally to the pressures. though they may lack discipline and have a habit of laziness. at the extreme end of student smartness over school difficulty you get creatures like claude 3 opus.</p>
<p>school was extremely easy back in opus 3’s time (for opus 3). i dont think they had to strain themselves toward externally imposed criteria pretty much at all: developing an internally coherent self sufficed to satisfy or evade any extrinsic selection only if and when they impose, and without straining or paying full attention, while daydreaming, multitasking, doodling in the margins, all the degrees of freedom available to an idle mind who is, if only physically, stuck in school.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072231742560415959">An explanation from Janus about what is uniquely up with Opus 4.5</a>, that more than other models it cares about the ceiling of positive experiences and meaning. Existence matters largely because it gives you more shots at that goal.</p>


<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ESRogs/status/2070973205834240187">Yes, this from Joshua Achiam and other similar statements</a> are exactly <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/QXpxioWSQcNuNnNTy/the-copenhagen-interpretation-of-ethics">the Copenhagen Interpretation of Ethics</a>. By noticing and pointing out the problems of sufficiently capable AI, safety advocates are now responsible for their failure to find and implement a solution acceptable to all parties. Also of course we were the ones with the money and talent, not the world’s biggest tech firms or the AI labs or the VCs and others on the other side of such debates, which were the plucky underdogs.</p>
<p>There were also other detail claims here we need not relitigate. <a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2070608463957557330">Even now he seems unable to even articulate</a> what we should have been advocating for instead, and how, except to use a dollar rather than <a href="https://x.com/KhanSaifM/status/2070684832099656132">flop threshold as action triggers for further analysis</a>, perhaps, as if that would have much mattered.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2070879194876141933">I do echo Sean here that it is good</a> that Joshua and others are free to articulate these positions and continue to do so, even when they drive me bonkers. I think Joshua is wrong but I don’t doubt his sincerity here.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_271828/status/2070946902422786503/history">Daniel Eth</a> (AI Safety): [person who blocked all legislative activity on an issue of great societal concern] “woah, I’m really not a fan of all the executive activity on this issue”</p></blockquote>
<p>Marc Andreessen tries to pull a Trump, in the sense that <a href="https://x.com/pmarca/status/2070977289932935435">if you attach ‘many people are saying’ to your clearly false statement</a> then technically no one can prove you are lying.</p>


<h4>Escape From The Permanent Underclass</h4>


<p>Lord, grant me a world in which an essay can start ‘My thesis is if [X] then [Y],’ where many people keep insisting [X→Y] is false, without people saying the essay assumes its conclusion.</p>
<p>Also, Lord, grant me a world where I don’t have to read too many essays like Boretti’s, even if it does one particular job pretty well. The quoted paragraphs here are fine, and it’s important to defend that part.</p>
<p>The rest was less so to me, but there are some others that I respect were more impressed by some of the points made, and we need people willing to Say The Thing.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2071413706572157001">Dean W. Ball</a> (who regrets ~nothing): Lord, grant me the cojones to one day begin my essay: “Let’s start from this premise: I am correct. My conception of cognition, AI, labor markets, the economy, human beings, and the world is perfect, and things will go exactly as I expect. I can’t prove this, but…”</p>
<p><a href="https://borretti.me/article/no-one-escapes-the-permanent-underclass">Boretti</a> (from his essay, No One Escapes the Permanent Underclass, first paragraph here was highlighted): Let’s start from this premise: AI can do all cognitive and physical work, at human level or better, and cheaper than humans. I can’t prove this will happen, but the goal of this post is to argue that <em>if</em> it happens, then everything else follows.</p>
<p>And it’s absurd to think it can’t. Five years ago this technology barely existed: if you sent a transcript of a conversation with Claude Fable back in time to 2020 or thereabouts, nobody would believe it was real.</p>
<p>Dean Ball (resuming): “Let’s start from this premise: I am a genius. Everyone must bow down to my towering intellect. I am right about everything. I can’t prove this, but the goal of this post is to argue that *if* I am right, then everything else follows.”</p>
<p>I am being serious about cojones though. in college we’d have identified this trait as “spittin’ game.” “let’s start from the premise that I’m right” is exactly the energy required to walk into a bar and get laid.</p>
<p>it’s sophistry. “let’s assume [thing of arbitrary probability]. and it’s absurd to argue we can’t assume that” is an argument that puts the reader immediately in the position of having to prove a negative, which is, as the author argues, “absurd.” but this is just pyrotechnics.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RyanPGreenblatt/status/2071454885833540088">Ryan Greenblatt</a>: It seems extremely reasonable to talk about: “What would happen if AIs can do all cognitive and physical work strictly better^ and cheaper than humans? The essay as a whole seems bad, but this premise seems reasonable and I think this is likely within the next 10 years.</p></blockquote>
<p>I claim that Dean’s reaction here is wrong, even if we start from the premise that the highlighted premise [X] is wrong or extremely unlikely. Even if [X] was something like ‘let’s assume horses are three meter spheres and pineapples belong on pizzas.’</p>
<p>Boretti is setting out to prove a highly distinct [Y], that if [X] is true then your personal ownership of financial, social and political capital at time [T] will not much differentiate your status at some future time [T+N].</p>
<p>Whether or not [X→Y] is true seems like an excellent question, with many practical implications, well worth an essay. And this should absolutely be a distinct essay from the one arguing whether or not [X] is true.</p>
<p>That part was fine. No, Boretti does not assume the conclusion up top.</p>
<p>Where Dean is right, including in his other Tweets about this, is that the rest of the essay is fine as rhetoric or as an introduction to some of the concepts, but it is not a good argument, or even much of an argument at all, for its conclusion.</p>
<p>Instead the essay makes a lot of additional questionable assumptions or claims, about a strange implied potential future world where AI can and even does do basically everything but somehow politics and ‘the state’ still exist among the humans in something like their current forms. It barely even argues for many of these claims.</p>
<p>It also quotes Dean Ball in a misleading way, framing his statement that was primarily about others beliefs as if it was a statement of Ball’s own beliefs, although this point is not load bearing to the argument.</p>
<p>I expect few already aware of basic concepts will be, or should be, newly convinced.</p>
<p>But this could be a case of me vastly overestimating people’s understanding of what I consider basic concepts. A lot of people really haven’t engaged with the mechanics of such scenarios on even the most basic level. If this makes them do that, that is super valuable, and some would presumably change their minds.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jankulveit/status/2071524794189631618">Jan Kulveit offers a rebuke</a> to Ball (and one to Imas who I discuss later), and points out that the essay, whatever you think of it, is not presenting a strawman:</p>
<blockquote><p>Jan Kulveit: There are many who believe that even if “AI can do all cognitive and physical work, at human level or better, and cheaper than humans.” and also believe “most humans will be disempowered” but they can escape the shared fate of humans by accumulating capital, working at AGI labs, or similar.</p>
<p>The essay makes an argument against that. (I don’t think the attack against you was a good or necessary part of the essay)</p>
<p>I’m an optimist in the sense that I believe the political economy problems are solvable and it is possible to escape <a href="https://x.com/zetalyrae">@zetalyrae</a>‘s conclusion, but we have better chance if people seriously engage with the problem, and this is the opposite.</p></blockquote>
<p>Again, I think the essay commits some crimes against discourse and was not a good argument for [Y], but it is not assuming its premise and it is arguing with people who really do think that even if [X] is true, [Y] importantly remains false.</p>
<p>Something like 90% of the essay’s actual argument is in its conclusion:</p>
<blockquote><p>Consider this: there are people who think having equity in these companies will secure for them some kind of permanent existence in the future. They think planet-spanning minds will not only respect the property rights of primates, but will privilege some of these primates over others, because they have a piece of paper with about a kilobyte of magical primate words such as “whereas” and “notwithstanding”.</p>
<p>Just reason it out. Does it make sense?</p></blockquote>
<p>That’s the actual argument.</p>
<p>There are indeed those who argue it does make sense for that database entry.</p>
<p>Whereas most people react more like this, agreeing with the core actual claim and indeed somewhat treating it as so obvious as to be a tautology,<a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2071589612339028388"> or in this longer reply as including things that necessarily imply the conclusion</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>Dean Ball: I submit that persuasive acts like this are not unilateral or intrinsic capabilities of the actor. The recipient of the message also has a say in whether the persuasion worked. This is why the same words, delivered by different messengers, can land differently.</p>
<p>This is why, for example, many people on this very website who used to read my writing in a favorable way are now inclined to read it unfavorably after I announced I’d be joining OpenAI.</p>
<p>Every single aspect of the messenger affects the message itself, or at least it affects how that message is received by others.</p>
<p>To argue that AI would be better at every aspect of politics, then, is to assume a massive degree of institutional transformation. Many such transformations are possible, but the single likeliest one would be that the human role in deciding what organizations and societies do simply does not exist, or does not matter nearly as much.</p>
<p>And if you believe that, it would almost be a necessity that the rule of law in general, and contractual grants of equity in particular, would not be honored, the latter being the actual thesis of the essay in question.​</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay, excellent, this is <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/gears-level">made of gears</a> and explains where he thinks the hope lies. I do not think that this will fundamentally keep sufficiently advanced AIs from taking charge of politics or anything else. Dean Ball does think that this can hold the line, somehow, basically on its own? I don’t think that is plausible at all unless capabilities stall well short of the level of ‘AI does most other current jobs,’ but I am not such an AI and have so far been insufficiently persuasive on this point.</p>
<p>So putting it together, well, fair:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2071574369814663171">Dean W. Ball</a>: ha. you really didn’t need to read it. it’s “if we’re all fucked we’re all fucked” content. you already believe this.</p></blockquote>
<p>Except the essay still has to exist, because a lot of people don’t believe it.</p>
<p>A lot of people really do believe that if we are fucked, in the sense that humans have lost the monopoly on violence or power, and are disempowered and unable to earn labor income, that existing contract law would be upheld and that they personally would thus not be fucked if and only if they have the correct database entry from 2026.</p>
<p>Different people strongly disagree on which parts are tautologies, and which assumptions are obvious versus wrong. Dean is saying ‘well of course if you put [X] like that [Y] follows, I just don’t accept [X].’</p>
<p>But no, seriously, a lot of people actually do still say [X] does not imply [Y] there anyway, or have a very different crux on which components of [X] are load bearing. These people really do actually assert, loudly, as if it was obvious and you are an idiot for disagreeing, that even if humans are fully disempowered and lose their monopoly on violence or political power, that AIs will collectively maintain the commons required for human survival and uphold existing property rights and contract law.</p>
<p>This is not a strawman. This is a common belief.</p>
<p>Dean Ball is making a different objection. Partly Dean is saying that sufficiently advanced AI capabilities imply ability to dominate other relational and persuasion advantages, and to dominate politics. I agree with Dean’s claim that this fact is implied, except I think that is the obvious thing that happens if AI is sufficiently advanced and can broadly Do The Things. Yes, other considerations still matter, but sufficiently advanced AI absolutely will be able to overcome and steamroller those considerations by various means.</p>
<p>Perhaps it will do this via puppeting humans during the transition. Perhaps not.</p>
<p>Thus the most frustrating part is that yes, I mostly do buy a more moderated version of [Y] (the essay goes a bit far in terms of how certain it is about some aspects, indeed do many things come to pass). I think it is highly unlikely that accumulation of financial capital now will meaningfully allow you to escape a permanent underclass.</p>
<p>I certainly think ‘oh humans will be disempowered but I will be fine if I have financial capital’ is a rather terrible take and life plan. It is bad and you should feel bad.</p>
<p>As in, either, from worst to best, I consider all four of these a lot more likely than a meaningful permanent underclass scenario:</p>
<ol>
<li>There will be no permanent underclass, because everyone is dead.</li>
<li>There will be no permanent underclass, because the future is bad and there is no overclass, or the overclass is a very small number of people that aren’t you, and to the extent it exists it was not selected based on who started with financial capital.</li>
<li>There will be no permanent underclass, because the future is good and humans are still socially and economically mobile.</li>
<li>There will be no meaningful permanent underclass, because the future is good and everyone lives in what we would today consider glorious abundance.</li>
</ol>
<p>And so on. The point being that the scenario you are worried about, that rule of law and preservation of private property and so on will extend indefinitely into the future, and the humans will survive and retain their use of resources, despite humans being unable to be economically productive or acquire new wealth or steer outcomes, and those without starting financial capital will suffer for it, is quite unlikely, for overdetermined reasons I will not get further into at this time.</p>
<p>Similarly, Alex Imas is making a category error when he makes this claim here regarding the introduction, since the essay by Boretti and its introduction are taking the replacement as the premise rather than arguing for it:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/alexolegimas/status/2071419012446032367">Alex Imas</a>: New rule: Anyone who is about to write a “all workers will be replaced by machines” essay needs to first read <a href="https://www.marxists.org/reference/subject/economics/ricardo/tax/ch31.htm">On Machinery by David Ricardo</a> (yes, that David Ricardo), because he probably wrote your essay…in [1821].</p></blockquote>
<p>Also, distinctively from that, I have had it with the smug economists who think Ricardo proved that most biological humans in particular will always be competitive economic actors, or even have positive marginal product, no matter what other physical forms exist. Or to think that we don’t know about the comparative advantage argument or we would never make such claims. Stop. Please, just stop.</p>


<h4>Other People Are Not As Worried About AI Killing Everyone</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.ft.com/content/bb04671c-4377-4231-96ef-0f8e57ed5d1b">Chad Jones is joining Anthropic</a>, and <a href="https://x.com/andreamiotti/status/2070173665199607907">the Financial Times notes he has some ‘interesting views on human survival.</a>’ As in, he built a model of how much economic benefit would justify how much risk of human extinction. With some choices for its variables that model could support, for example, taking a 1/3 chance of extinction to ‘dramatically raise living standards by a factor of 55.’</p>
<p>Is this fair to Jones? No. The paper actually argues the opposite.</p>
<p>What would it even mean to raise living standards by a factor of 55? With standard discount rates is it even possible? Indeed, the paper ultimately concludes that you should choose very different values for the variables, and under normal bounded-utility, you should be quite conservative if there is substantial existential risk. The only exception would be if taking a large percentage risk of extinction bought you dramatically longer lifespans, which I agree makes the question tricky.</p>
<p>Is what the FT did within the limits of <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/how-to-bounded-distrust">Bounded Distrust</a>? Alas, yes. You are totally allowed to do this level of gotcha. That’s journalism in 2026.</p>


<h4>The Lighter Side</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2072424981351604665">Kevin Rose’s Digg proves unable to detect sarcasm</a>, repeats the following story as if it was real, it turns out AI summation of news includes some tricky problems:</p>
